#### `tests-b-5` — T15.8 never establishes its precondition, so all four keyboard-takeover assertions pass on a chart where quick navigation is already off

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T15_Ux.mq5:421`** · category: event-handling

```mql5
      long was_nav = ChartGetInteger(chart, CHART_QUICK_NAVIGATION);
      long was_key = ChartGetInteger(chart, CHART_KEYBOARD_CONTROL);
      ...
      CheckEq("quick navigation is off while the panel lives",
              0, ChartGetInteger(chart, CHART_QUICK_NAVIGATION));
      ...
      CheckEq("quick navigation is given back",
              was_nav, ChartGetInteger(chart, CHART_QUICK_NAVIGATION));
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: The test is not unconditionally vacuous - on a default chart (quick navigation and keyboard control both on) all four assertions do bite. The defect is the missing precondition: it never asserts or forces was_nav/was_key == 1, so the section silently stops guarding on any chart where they are already off (including one left that way by an earlier T15.8 run that died between Create and Destroy).

**Why:** The section reads the current values into was_nav/was_key and never forces them to a known state (no ChartSetInteger(..., true) before Create). CSSRPanel::Create saves them (SSR_Panel.mqh:320-321) and writes false (343-344); Destroy writes the saved values back (372-373). With was_nav == 0 the first CheckEq compares 0 against a value that was already 0, and the restore CheckEq compares 0 against 0. This is exactly the regression class the section was added for (the comment: "Fifteen phases and 1,159 assertions never saw it"), so a guard that can silently stop guarding matters here.

**Verifiers:** Traced the section at SSR_T15_Ux.mq5:419-441. It reads was_nav/was_key with ChartGetInteger and never forces them to a known state before panel.Create - no ChartSetInteger(..., true) anywhere in the section. CSSRPanel::Create saves both at SSR_Panel.mqh:320-321 and writes false at l.343-344; Destroy restores them at l.372-373. So with was_nav/was_key already 0 the first two CheckEq compare 0 against an already-0 property and would pass with lines 343-344 deleted, and the restore pair compares 0 against 0 - both exactly as claimed. The described defect (missing precondition) is provable from source; the vacuous-pass outcome is conditional on chart state, and MT5 charts ship with quick navigation and keyboard control ON, so on a default chart the guard still bites. LOW rather than the finder's MEDIUM: a conditionally weak test, no product impact / The structural half is source-provable: T15.8 reads was_nav/was_key and never forces them true before panel.Create (SSR_T15_Ux.mq5:421-427), while Create saves then writes false (SSR_Panel.mqh:316-344) and Destroy writes the saved pair back (:366-375). But the claimed consequence - all four assertions passing vacuously, and the restore pair being unable to distinguish 'restored' from 'wrote the same constant' - only materialises on a chart where CHART_QUICK_NAVIGATION and CHART_KEYBOARD_CONTROL are ALREADY off. Both default to enabled in MetaTrader, so on a normal chart the CheckEq(0, ...) pair genuinely exercises the two ChartSetInteger lines and the restore pair genuinely compares 1 against 1. Whether a given terminal/template/aborted prior run leaves them off is runtime state this source cannot settle, so per lens this is at most POTENTIAL_RISK: a fragile precondition rather than a proven dead assertion / The section does read the current values into was_nav/was_key and never forces a known state before panel.Create (SSR_T15_Ux.mq5:421-422), while Create saves-then-writes false (SSR_Panel.mqh:319-320, 343-344) and Destroy restores (365-373), so on a chart that already has both off the four assertions pass vacuously and the restore pair degenerates to 0 == 0. Downgraded from MEDIUM: MetaTrader ships both chart properties enabled, so on a normal chart the first two assertions do bite and the restore comparison against was_nav == 1 genuinely distinguishes restore from a hardcoded write. The blind spot needs an unusual chart state (user-disabled, or a panel that died without Destroy), and the consequence is a weaker test, never a user-visible fault

#### `tests-a-8` — "warmup survived the reset" is measured with a write counter that a wipe cannot decrement

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T1_CoreEngine.mq5:355`** · category: future-data-leakage

```mql5
      int seeded = sink.SeedBarCount();
      Check("reset succeeded", ctrl.Reset());
...
      CheckEq("warmup survived the reset", seeded, sink.SeedBarCount());
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: Accurate, but the category is wrong: this is a test-coverage/assertion-strength gap, not 'future-data-leakage'. Scope note: the in-memory recording sink stores no bars at all, so the uncovered risk is specific to the MT5 custom-symbol sink path, which this test does not exercise.

**Why:** Core/Sinks/SSR_RecordingSink.mqh:71 is `m_seed_bars += count;` - the only write to that field outside `Clear()`. `TruncateFrom` (line 103-120) rewrites `m_ticks`, `m_tick_count` and `m_last_msc` and leaves `m_seed_bars` alone. `CSSRReplayController::Reset` (SSR_ReplayController.mqh:1276-1278) calls `m_sink.TruncateFrom(m_timeline.start_msc)` then `m_sink.OnReset()`, and `OnReset` is `{ m_resets++; }`. The sink exposes no live bar count - `FirstTickMsc`, `LastTickMsc`, `TickAt` and `CountAfter` all read the tick array - so no assertion in the current sink API could distinguish a surviving warmup from a deleted one.

**Verifiers:** Traced the sink and the controller. CSSRRecordingSink::SeedBars (MQL5/Include/SSReplay/Core/Sinks/SSR_RecordingSink.mqh:69-73) is `m_seed_bars += count;` -- a cumulative write counter, and Clear() at 45-60 is the only other write. TruncateFrom (103-120) rewrites m_ticks/m_tick_count/m_last_msc and never touches m_seed_bars. CSSRReplayController::Reset (SSR_ReplayController.mqh:1268-1297) calls m_sink.TruncateFrom(m_timeline.start_msc) then m_sink.OnReset(), and OnReset is `{ m_resets++; }` (RecordingSink.mqh:125); nothing in Reset re-seeds. So SSR_T1_CoreEngine.mq5:355 `CheckEq("warmup survived the reset", seeded, sink.SeedBarCount())` can only fail on spurious re-seeding, never on warmup deletion. The sink's public surface (lines 129-145: TickCount, SeedBarCount, Truncations, Seeks, Resets, FirstTickMsc, LastTickMsc, TickAt, CountAfter) exposes no live bar count, so the claim that no assertion in the current API could detect a deleted warmup holds. SSR_T8:315-320 repeats the same pattern for Restart, as claimed / Verified: CSSRRecordingSink::SeedBars is `m_seed_bars += count;` (SSR_RecordingSink.mqh:69-72) and m_seed_bars is written nowhere else except Clear(). TruncateFrom (102-121) rewrites m_ticks/m_tick_count/m_last_msc only. CSSRReplayController::Reset (SSR_ReplayController.mqh:1268-1297) calls m_sink.TruncateFrom(m_timeline.start_msc) then m_sink.OnReset(), and OnReset is `{ m_resets++; }`. So `CheckEq("warmup survived the reset", seeded, sink.SeedBarCount())` compares a monotone write counter against itself; it can only fail on extra re-seeding. T8.8:315-320 is the same pattern for Restart / The mechanics are exactly as stated: CSSRRecordingSink::SeedBars is `m_seed_bars += count;` (SSR_RecordingSink.mqh:71), TruncateFrom (103-119) rewrites m_ticks/m_tick_count/m_last_msc and never touches m_seed_bars, and only Clear() resets it. So CheckEq("warmup survived the reset", seeded, sink.SeedBarCount()) at T1:355 cannot fail if Reset deleted the warmup - it only catches the opposite fault, spurious re-seeding. But the impact claim overreaches. The finder's own category 'future-data-leakage' is wrong; nothing leaks. And the production risk is close to unreachable: CSSRReplayController::Reset truncates from m_timeline.start_msc (SSR_ReplayController.mqh:1277), and warmup bars by definition precede start_msc, so a real sink cuts nothing from the warmup; the 'higher timeframes left empty' scenario has no code path behind it. This is a weak-assertion / test-sensitivity issue, not a defect with a user-observable consequence

#### `tests-a-9` — T2.6's stated dangling-guard test never lets the guard die

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T2_DataEngine.mq5:297`** · category: object-lifecycle

```mql5
      //--- TODO T1 from Phase 1: detaching must leave nothing pointing
      //--- at a guard that is about to die
      src.DetachGuard();
      MqlRates after[];
      int na = bp.ReadBars(sym, win_start + SSR_MSC_PER_MIN,
                                win_start + 5 * SSR_MSC_PER_MIN, after);
      Check("reads still work after detach", na >= 0, IntegerToString(na));
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: T2.6's post-detach assertion cannot fail either way: guard refusal returns 0 (SSR_Mt5Providers.mqh:259-263), so `na >= 0` holds whether or not DetachGuard() cleared the provider's pointer. The dangling-guard lifetime is traversed unasserted in T2.9 after the guard's scope closes; to actually test detach, assert na > 0 after the detach (or compare guard.Violations() before and after).

**Why:** T2:267 `CSSRFutureGuard guard;` is a stack object whose scope is the `else` block opened at T2:265 and closed at T2:304, three lines after the read. `src` is declared far outside at T2:183, so the two objects' lifetimes are the wrong way round for the stated test: the guard dies first only after the section is over, at which point nothing reads through `src` again until T2.9 - which would crash rather than fail if DetachGuard were broken. To actually test it, the guard must go out of scope (or be `new`/`delete`d) before the post-detach read.

**Verifiers:** Lifetimes traced and the claim holds. `CSSRFutureGuard guard;` is declared at T2:267 as a stack object inside the `else` block opened at :265 and closed at :304; DetachGuard() is at :299 and the read at :301, both while `guard` is still alive, so no dangling pointer can be dereferenced. The assertion is also insensitive to the behaviour it names: CSSRMt5BarProvider::ReadBars (SSR_Mt5Providers.mqh:259-263) returns 0 via `Succeed()` when GuardRange rejects the range, so if DetachGuard were a no-op the still-armed guard would refuse the pure-future window and na would be 0 — `na >= 0` passes either way, exactly as it passes when detach works and bars come back. `src` is declared at T2:183, outside the block, so the two lifetimes are inverted relative to the hazard the comment describes. The real path (source outliving the guard, handled by ~CSSRMt5DataSource calling DetachGuard at SSR_Mt5DataSource.mqh:43) is exercised nowhere in T1..T8. Severity lowered from MEDIUM: the production lifetime handling is correct — DetachGuard only nulls pointers, it never dereferences — so this is purely an untested-path gap, not a live object-lifecycle defect / The assertion is provably non-discriminating. CSSRMt5BarProvider::ReadBars (SSR_Mt5Providers.mqh:259-263) returns 0 - not -1 - when GuardRange refuses the range, and T2:282 already pins that behaviour. So at T2:301-303 na is 0 if DetachGuard did nothing (guard still armed at win_start, range wholly in the future) and > 0 if it worked; `na >= 0` passes in both cases, testing only 'no crash', never the detach. The guard object is still in scope (declared T2:267, block closes T2:304), so the stated use-after-free is not exercised at the read. One claim in the evidence is wrong: the post-scope dangling path IS traversed later - T2.9 (:416-444) calls src.Mt5Bars().ReadBars after the guard's block has closed - it is simply unasserted and would manifest as a crash or garbage rather than a red check, which is also runtime behaviour I cannot settle from source / Scope proves it. `CSSRFutureGuard guard;` is a stack object of the else block opened at T2:265 and closed at T2:304, and the post-detach read is at T2:301, so the guard is still alive at the read and the stated use-after-free lifetime is never created. The check is weaker still than the finding says: `na >= 0` passes whether DetachGuard worked (guard gone, future bars returned, na > 0) or did nothing (guard still armed, refusal, na == 0), so it does not even verify detachment, only that the call did not error. The destructor path that exists for this hazard (SSR_Mt5DataSource.mqh:43-44) is untested, and in the product the guard is a by-value member of the controller (SSR_ReplayController.mqh:64) whose DetachGuard calls are at :559/:569/:1307 - so the ordering question is real but unexercised. Impact is nonetheless test-coverage only: no defect in the guard itself is shown, and the future-leak protection is asserted positively elsewhere in the same section (T2:280-295). MEDIUM overstates a missing test; LOW

#### `tests-a-11` — T2.8 closes with an unconditional `Check(..., true)`

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T2_DataEngine.mq5:406`** · category: test-coverage

```mql5
         //--- Phase 1 said Core would not change to accept a real source.
         //--- This is that claim, executed.
         Check("Phase 1 contract held with a real source", true);
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: Correct and cosmetic: the line is a no-op assertion that bumps g_pass and prints a PASS a reader may take as verified. Note that the claim it names is in fact demonstrated by the block it sits in (the unmodified controller compiling and replaying through the real source), so the fix is to delete the line or replace it with a comment, not to add coverage. IMPROVEMENT.

**Why:** The argument is the literal `true`, so `ok` is always true and the `g_fail` branch is unreachable. Nothing between line 404 and 406 computes a value that is consulted. This is the only unconditional `Check` in T1..T8.

**Verifiers:** T2:406 is literally `Check("Phase 1 contract held with a real source", true);`. Check (T2:32-34) increments g_pass and prints "PASS <name>" whenever ok is true, so the g_fail branch is unreachable and the line asserts nothing while printing a sentence that reads as a verified architectural claim. Nothing between :402 and :406 computes a consulted value. `grep -n 'Check("[^"]*", *true)' MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/*.mq5` matches this line and only this line across the whole test suite, so the finding's uniqueness claim is also correct. Inflates the PASS count by one; harmless otherwise / Verified literally: T2:406 is Check("Phase 1 contract held with a real source", true) with the constant `true` as the predicate, so g_fail can never increment and the line only inflates g_pass and prints a PASS for an architectural claim nothing evaluated. Nothing between :402 and :406 computes a consulted value. Confirmed unique across the suite - a grep for Check("...", true) over MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/ returns this line only. Test-hygiene defect, no runtime dependency; severity LOW / Trivially provable: the argument is the literal `true`, the g_fail branch is unreachable, and a grep of MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests confirms it is the only unconditional Check in the suite. What it inflates, though, is one line of a developer script's PASS count and one sentence in its log. There is also a defensible reading that softens it: the substance of "Core did not change to accept a real source" is proved by the surrounding block compiling and running CSSRReplayController unmodified against CSSRMt5DataSource, which the twelve preceding assertions exercise - the line is a documentation marker attached to the wrong tool. Zero user impact, zero product behaviour, cosmetic report inflation: IMPROVEMENT rather than LOW

#### `tests-a-7` — T3.8 "teardown leaves nothing behind" runs against a symbol T3.7 already deleted

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T3_CustomSymbol.mq5:441`** · category: object-lifecycle

```mql5
      mgr.Destroy();
      SSRPause(300);

      string sym = SSRReplaySymbolName(origin, InpSlot);
      ResetLastError();
      long d = SymbolInfoInteger(sym, SYMBOL_DIGITS);
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: Correct for the normal path. One caveat the finding omits: if a.Create at T3:418 fails, the Skip branch runs, second.Destroy() never executes, and mgr's symbol is still present -- so T3.8 is meaningful only in the failure path it was not written for. In the passing path it is vacuous, exactly as described.

**Why:** `mgr.Create(origin, InpSlot)` (T3:99) and `a.Create(origin, InpSlot)` (T3:418) both set `m_symbol = SSRReplaySymbolNameFor(origin, slot, m_anonymous)` (SSR_CustomSymbolManager.mqh:307), so mgr, a and second all name the same symbol; T3:427 asserts exactly that (`second.Symbol() == a.Symbol()`). T3:434 calls `second.Destroy()`, which reaches `CustomSymbolDelete(m_symbol)` (SSR_CustomSymbolManager.mqh:553). `Destroy()` opens with `if(m_symbol == "") return true;` and ends with `Succeed(); return ok;` - deleting an absent symbol is explicitly "not a failure" per its own comment - so mgr.Destroy() is a silent no-op here and the assertion `e != 0 || d == 0` reads a symbol that has been absent since T3.7.

**Verifiers:** Traced the naming and lifecycle. SetAnonymous is never called in SSR_T3_CustomSymbol.mq5, so m_anonymous is false for every manager (SSR_CustomSymbolManager.mqh:220) and SSRReplaySymbolNameFor(origin, slot, false) returns SSRReplaySymbolName(origin, slot) (SSR_SymbolNaming.mqh:66-71) -- mgr (T3:99), a (T3:418) and second (T3:425) therefore all carry the identical m_symbol, which T3:427 itself asserts. T3:434 calls second.Destroy(), which reaches CustomSymbolDelete(m_symbol) at SSR_CustomSymbolManager.mqh:544-556, so the symbol is gone when T3.8 begins. T3:441 mgr.Destroy() then re-runs CloseCharts/SymbolSelect(false)/CustomSymbolDelete on an absent name, its return value discarded, and the assertion at 448 (`e != 0 || d == 0` after SymbolInfoInteger on a nonexistent symbol) passes for a reason unrelated to teardown. OpenChartCount() at 450 likewise returns 0 trivially. No intervening guard recreates the symbol between 434 and 441 / T3.7 (SSR_T3_CustomSymbol.mq5:414-435) ends with second.Destroy(); a.Create and mgr.Create both resolve m_symbol via SSRReplaySymbolNameFor(origin, slot, m_anonymous) (SSR_CustomSymbolManager.mqh:307), and T3:427 asserts second.Symbol() == a.Symbol(). Destroy (SSR_CustomSymbolManager.mqh:543-586) closes charts, deselects, then CustomSymbolDelete, so the symbol is gone before T3.8 runs; mgr.Destroy() at line 441 is then a no-op whose return value is discarded, and SymbolInfoInteger on an absent symbol yields d==0 with a non-zero error, satisfying `e != 0 || d == 0` regardless. I also checked that no other section could catch a Destroy regression: Create() explicitly adopts a pre-existing replay symbol when CustomSymbolCreate fails (lines 328-348), so T3.7's 'second create succeeds over the leftover' would still pass with Destroy stubbed out / Traceable end to end. mgr, a and second all call Create(origin, InpSlot) and set m_symbol = SSRReplaySymbolNameFor(origin, slot, m_anonymous) (SSR_CustomSymbolManager.mqh:307), and T3:427 asserts second.Symbol() == a.Symbol(). T3.7's last statement is second.Destroy() (T3:434), which reaches CustomSymbolDelete (line ~550). So by T3.8 the symbol is already absent, mgr.Destroy() is effectively a no-op whose bool return is discarded, and both assertions - `e != 0 || d == 0` and OpenChartCount()==0 (which can only count charts on a symbol that no longer exists) - pass regardless of what Destroy does. Impact is bounded, though: a regressed Destroy would leave a custom symbol behind, and the product already treats that as survivable - Create() calls Destroy() then adopts a leftover (T3.7 is the test for exactly that), and SSR_Z_Cleanup exists (T3:456). So the consequence of the blind spot is a stale symbol, not a corrupted session. LOW rather than MEDIUM

#### `tests-a-5` — T5.6's "foreign objects ignored" is now a vacuous pass - every object click is ignored

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T5_Ui.mq5:221`** · category: event-handling

```mql5
      Check("foreign objects ignored",
            !panel.OnEvent(CHARTEVENT_OBJECT_CLICK, l, d, foreign));
      CheckEq("nothing was sent", 0, port.play + port.pause + port.reset);
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: Confirmed. It is one half of the same coverage hole as tests-a-1; the assertion is tautological rather than wrong, and its only cost is a maintainer believing the prefix filter is covered when it is not.

**Why:** Same evidence as tests-a-1: CHARTEVENT_OBJECT_CLICK appears only in the comment at SSR_Panel.mqh:2878; OnEvent's trailing `return false` (line 2971) is the only path an object-click event can take. The prefix filter that the assertion means to exercise lives in `PollClicks` (SSR_Panel.mqh:2480, `if(StringFind(name, m_prefix) != 0) continue;`), which the test never calls.

**Verifiers:** Same trace as tests-a-1 and it holds: with no CHARTEVENT_OBJECT_CLICK branch, OnEvent returns false for every object click, so `!panel.OnEvent(CHARTEVENT_OBJECT_CLICK, l, d, "SomeUserObject")` is true unconditionally and port.play+pause+reset is 0 unconditionally. Both assertions are vacuous passes that cannot fail, and the prefix filter they are meant to exercise lives in PollClicks (the `if(StringFind(name, m_prefix) != 0) continue;` guard), which the test never invokes. The characterisation of this as the quieter half of tests-a-1 is fair. Downgraded to LOW: it is a coverage illusion in a test file, with no product defect behind it / Same source fact as tests-a-1, verified independently: every CHARTEVENT_OBJECT_CLICK falls through OnEvent to `return false` (SSR_Panel.mqh:2971), so `!panel.OnEvent(CHARTEVENT_OBJECT_CLICK, ...)` is true for the panel's own buttons as much as for 'SomeUserObject', and port.play+pause+reset is 0 because nothing was dispatched. Both assertions pass and neither can fail, so the section proves nothing about the prefix filter it names - that filter is `if(StringFind(name, m_prefix) != 0) continue;` inside PollClicks (SSR_Panel.mqh:2480), which the test never calls. The 'would stay green if a future click branch dispatched without a prefix check' argument is sound as long as such a branch returned true only for dispatched names, which is the likely shape. Severity lowered from MEDIUM to LOW: a vacuous assertion is a coverage illusion, not a defect that can harm a run / Same source basis as tests-a-1, and the vacuity argument holds: OnEvent falls through to return false (line 2971) for every object click, so !OnEvent(...) is true for the panel's own buttons as well as a foreign name, and port counters cannot move because no dispatch exists. Both assertions pass for the wrong reason and cannot fail. The prefix filter they are meant to prove lives in PollClicks (2480). Impact is a false green in a test script - misleading to a maintainer, invisible to a user - so LOW rather than MEDIUM

#### `tests-a-6` — T5.9 (second) asserts on the test double instead of the panel for JumpTo and Restart

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T5_Ui.mq5:318`** · category: event-handling

```mql5
      Check("the port can be jumped", p59.JumpTo(123456789));
      CheckEq("which was recorded",   123456789, p59.last_jump_msc);
      Check("and restarted", p59.Restart());
      CheckEq("counted", 1, p59.restart);
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: The four JumpTo/Restart assertions provide no runtime coverage of the panel and are mislabelled by the section header "the navigation verbs reach the port"; their actual, documented purpose (T5:291-295) is a compile/link-time guard ensuring CFakePort still implements the Phase 8 port verbs. The real gap is that the panel-side paths for SSR_CMD_RESTART (reachable only via the "restart" button in PollClicks, SSR_Panel.mqh:2716) and SSR_CMD_JUMP (host-owned) have no panel-side test at all.

**Why:** CFakePort's bodies are T5:67-69. `CSSRPanel::Owns` (SSR_Panel.mqh, the switch listing SSR_CMD_NONE/SESSIONS/JUMP/REVIEW/REPLAY_FROM_HERE) returns false for SSR_CMD_JUMP, so a J keypress is deliberately not handled by the panel and cannot be tested this way at all; SSR_CMD_RESTART appears in no `SSRAddKey` call in SSR_Keys.mqh, so no key reaches it either. The two assertions immediately above (lines 310-316, LEFT and PgUp) do go through `panel59.OnEvent` and are sound - the contrast is within the same block.

**Verifiers:** The factual core holds. T5:318-321 calls p59.JumpTo/p59.Restart directly; those bodies are declared at T5:67-69 as `{ jump++; last_jump_msc = m; return true; }` and `{ restart++; return true; }`, so all four assertions are self-satisfying and nothing traverses panel59. The supporting claims check out too: CSSRPanel::Owns (SSR_Panel.mqh:2165-2176) lists SSR_CMD_JUMP among the commands it returns false for, so a J keypress is handed to the host and cannot be driven through the panel by OnEvent; and SSR_CMD_RESTART appears in no SSRAddKey call in SSR_Keys.mqh, so no keypress reaches ExecuteInner's `case SSR_CMD_RESTART: return m_port.Restart();` (SSR_Panel.mqh:2297). The contrast with the sound LEFT/PgUp assertions at 310-316 is real. Severity lowered to LOW and the description needs one correction (below): the file's own comment at 291-295 states the intended purpose is a LINK-time guard against CFakePort going abstract again, which these four lines do serve - they are not purposeless, only mislabelled by the section header / The four assertions do only exercise the double. CFakePort::JumpTo is `{ jump++; last_jump_msc = m; return true; }` (T5:67-68) and Restart is `{ restart++; return true; }` (T5:69), both called directly on p59 at T5:318-321, so they cannot fail and panel59 is not on the path. The two supporting facts hold as well: Owns() lists SSR_CMD_JUMP among the commands the panel refuses (SSR_Panel.mqh:2165-2177), so a J keydown returns false before Execute and cannot be tested through OnEvent; and no SSRAddKey call binds SSR_CMD_RESTART anywhere in SSR_Keys.mqh, so no key reaches `case SSR_CMD_RESTART: return m_port.Restart();` (SSR_Panel.mqh:2288). The contrast with the sound LEFT/PgUp assertions above (T5:308-316, which do route through panel59.OnEvent -> Owns -> ExecuteInner -> StepBack) is real. One correction to the finding's framing: the section's own stated purpose (T5:291-295) is link-time - forcing CFakePort to implement the port's pure virtuals - and calling the methods is not what achieves that; merely instantiating CFakePort is, since Name/IsConnected/ReadState/Play/.../Restart are all `= 0` in CSSRReplayPort (SSR_ReplayPort.mqh:309-327). So the assertions are redundant even for their declared reason. Severity lowered to LOW: a coverage gap in a test, nothing more / T5:318-321 call p59.JumpTo / p59.Restart directly, and CFakePort's bodies (T5:67-69) are `{ jump++; last_jump_msc = m; return true; }` and `{ restart++; return true; }` - so all four assertions test the double, not the panel. The surrounding claims check out: Owns() (SSR_Panel.mqh:2165-2177) returns false for SSR_CMD_JUMP, so a J keypress is deliberately passed to the host and cannot be driven through the panel, and no SSRAddKey binds SSR_CMD_RESTART, so `case SSR_CMD_RESTART: return m_port.Restart();` (2290) has no key-reachable coverage. Impact: false coverage in a test script, under a section heading that overpromises. No product behaviour is wrong; the compile-time value the test comment claims (the fake must implement the verbs or the class is abstract) comes from the class declaration, not from these lines

#### `tests-a-14` — T6.8's closing assertion is a tautology given the two above it

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T6_History.mq5:300`** · category: test-coverage

```mql5
      Check("and it differs from the flat default or matches deliberately",
            v.SessionGap() >= before);
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: Accurate, and the floor at SSR_DataValidator.mqh:124-126 makes it unconditional rather than merely 'implied by the two lines above': the preceding assertion at T6:290 (`gap >= SSR_SESSION_GAP_MSC`) is itself incapable of failing for the same reason, so both lines are tautologies, not just the last one.

**Why:** T6:289-299 sets `long gap = SSRSymbolSessionGap(origin)`, asserts `gap >= SSR_SESSION_GAP_MSC`, then `long before = v.SessionGap()` on a default-constructed `CSSRDataValidator`, then `v.LearnFrom(origin)` and `v.SessionGap() == gap`. The remaining condition reduces to `gap >= SSR_SESSION_GAP_MSC`, which line 290 already asserts.

**Verifiers:** Confirmed and in fact stronger than stated. CSSRDataValidator's constructor is `m_session_gap_msc(SSR_SESSION_GAP_MSC)` (MQL5/Include/SSReplay/Data/SSR_DataValidator.mqh:194) and SSR_SESSION_GAP_MSC is 60*60*1000 (line 22), so `before` at SSR_T6_History.mq5:296 is exactly that constant. LearnFrom (line 200) sets m_session_gap_msc = SSRSymbolSessionGap(symbol), and that function (lines 79-127) ends with `if(widest < SSR_SESSION_GAP_MSC) widest = SSR_SESSION_GAP_MSC;` and also returns SSR_SESSION_GAP_MSC when the symbol declares no sessions -- so its result is unconditionally >= before. Therefore T6:300-301 `v.SessionGap() >= before` cannot fail for any symbol or broker, and the assertion name ('differs from the flat default or matters deliberately') is an explicit A-or-not-A. The section also never compares two symbols, so the question of whether a 24/5 forex symbol and a session-broken index learn different gaps is indeed unasked / Verified at SSR_T6_History.mq5:283-300 and SSR_DataValidator.mqh:194-200. A default-constructed CSSRDataValidator has m_session_gap_msc == SSR_SESSION_GAP_MSC, so `before` is that constant; line 289-291 already asserts gap >= SSR_SESSION_GAP_MSC and 297-298 asserts v.SessionGap() == gap, so `v.SessionGap() >= before` adds nothing. SSRSymbolSessionGap itself is floored at SSR_SESSION_GAP_MSC (lines 124-125) and returns that constant when no sessions are declared (line 107), so all three checks are satisfied by construction on any symbol, and the section never compares a 24/5 symbol against a session-broken one / The implication chain holds exactly as claimed. CSSRDataValidator's default ctor sets m_session_gap_msc = SSR_SESSION_GAP_MSC (SSR_DataValidator.mqh:194), so `before` is that floor; SSRSymbolSessionGap floors its result at the same constant (lines 106-107, 124-125, and the n==0 fallback), so T6:290's gap >= SSR_SESSION_GAP_MSC is unconditionally true; T6:298 pins v.SessionGap() == gap; therefore v.SessionGap() >= before at line 300-301 is entailed and cannot fail independently. The name really does read 'A or not-A'. But the impact is nil - it is a redundant passing line beside two assertions that do work, it misleads nobody at runtime, and the deeper gap the finder names (never comparing a 24/5 forex symbol against a session-broken index) is a suggestion for new coverage, not a defect. IMPROVEMENT, below the finder's LOW

#### `tests-a-12` — T7.1 asserts arithmetic over its own literals and reads no production code

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T7_Performance.mq5:66`** · category: test-coverage

```mql5
      double ticks_per_bar = 8.0;
      double at_50x = (50.0 / 60.0) * ticks_per_bar;
      Check("50x needs under 10 ticks/sec", at_50x < 10.0,
            StringFormat("%.2f", at_50x));
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: One factual correction to the evidence: the ladder does not top out at 200x. Index 18 is 20000 (200x) and index 19 returns SSR_SPEED_MAX, which is 100000 in SSR_Types.mqh:131 — i.e. 1000x. So the narrative 'a day at 50x still takes over 25 minutes, which is why Jump exists rather than a bigger number' is stale by a factor of twenty, not four.

**Why:** Lines 66-79 declare `ticks_per_bar`, `at_50x`, `need_speed` and `day_minutes` from constants written in the same statements; no SSR_* macro, enum or function appears in any of the three conditions. The current ladder is Common/SSR_Types.mqh:275-302: index 15 is 5000 (50x), index 18 is 20000 (200x), index 19 is SSR_SPEED_MAX. Written against the real constants - `SSRSpeedLadder(SSR_SPEED_LADDER_SIZE-1)` and the default of `InpTicksPerBar` - the same three lines would have caught the ladder change that tests-a-2 and tests-a-3 missed.

**Verifiers:** Verified both halves. SSR_T7_Performance.mq5:66-79 builds all three conditions from literals declared in the same statements -- ticks_per_bar = 8.0 (66), at_50x = (50.0/60.0)*ticks_per_bar (67), need_speed = (2000.0/ticks_per_bar)*60.0 (71), day_minutes = 86400.0/50.0/60.0 (77) -- and no SSR_* macro, enum or function appears in any of them, so each Check is arithmetic over its own constants and cannot detect a product change. The staleness is real: SSRSpeedLadder (MQL5/Include/SSReplay/Common/SSR_Types.mqh:275-301) puts 50x (5000) at index 15, 100x at 17, 200x at 18 and SSR_SPEED_MAX at 19, with SSR_SPEED_MAX = 100000 (line 131) i.e. 1000x, and SSR_SPEED_LADDER_SIZE = 20 (line 302). So 50x is not the fastest setting and the comment's conclusion at 75-76 ('which is why Jump exists rather than a bigger number') no longer follows -- at the ladder top a day takes 1.4 minutes, not 28.8. InpTicksPerBar's default is still 8 (SSReplayStandalone.mq5:75), so the 8.0 literal happens to agree today, which is exactly the coupling the assertions fail to express / SSR_T7_Performance.mq5:66-79: at_50x, need_speed and day_minutes are computed entirely from literals declared in the same statements (8.0, 50.0, 60.0, 2000.0, 86400.0); no SSR_* constant, enum or function appears in any of the three conditions, so all three are arithmetic identities that cannot detect any product change. The staleness is real: SSRSpeedLadder (SSR_Types.mqh:275-300) now runs to index 19, and InpTicksPerBar's default lives in SSReplayStandalone.mq5:75 — neither is referenced here / Both parts verified. T7:66-79 builds at_50x, need_speed and day_minutes entirely from literals written in the same statements (8.0, 50.0, 60.0, 2000.0, 86400.0); no SSR_* symbol appears in any of the three conditions, so none can fail or detect a change. And the narrative is stale: SSRSpeedLadder (SSR_Types.mqh:275-301) puts 5000 (50x) at index 15, 20000 (200x) at 18 and SSR_SPEED_MAX (100000 = 1000x, SSR_Types.mqh:131) at 19, so 50x is no longer the fastest setting and 'a day at 50x still takes over 25 minutes, which is why Jump exists rather than a bigger number' no longer describes the product - at the ladder top a day is ~1.4 minutes. (InpTicksPerBar's default is still 8, SSReplayStandalone.mq5:75, so that literal is currently accurate.) Impact is documentation-grade: three always-green lines that assert arithmetic about themselves, plus a comment that could mislead a maintainer about the speed ceiling. Nothing a user can reach, so IMPROVEMENT rather than LOW

#### `tests-a-17` — T8.3's headline assertion admits any tick count from 1 upward

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T8_Navigation.mq5:148`** · category: r-multiple

```mql5
      Check("the tick count came BACK, not to zero",
            ctrl.TicksEmitted() > 0,
...
      Check("and it is near where it was",
            ctrl.TicksEmitted() <= mark_ticks,
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: T8.3's rewind assertions bound the restored tick counter only by `> 0` and `<= mark_ticks`, so any shortfall from a partial snapshot restore passes. The finding's arithmetic is wrong: Wire() sets SetTicksPerBar(8) and SSR_SPEED_1 is 1x, so 900 x Pump(1000) advances 900 s = 15 M1 bars and mark_ticks is on the order of 120 ticks, not the ~7000 the finding states - the admitted window is [1, ~120], not [1, ~7000]. The defect (no quantified 'near') stands; the size does not.

**Why:** `mark_ticks = ctrl.TicksEmitted()` is captured at T8:133 after 900 x `ctrl.Pump(1000)`; the rewind is `ctrl.StepBackward((int)back_bars)` with `back_bars = (ctrl.Now() - mark_time) / SSR_MSC_PER_MIN`, i.e. exactly 300 bars, so the post-rewind count should land within one checkpoint interval of `mark_ticks`. `CSSRSnapshotStore::SetInterval` is used in T8.2 with 60000 ms, so the tolerance is computable; the assertion instead uses 0 and mark_ticks as its bounds. A `Check(mark_ticks - ctrl.TicksEmitted() < <one interval worth of ticks>)` is what the section's own prose describes.

**Verifiers:** The assertion pair at T8:148-155 is exactly `ctrl.TicksEmitted() > 0` and `ctrl.TicksEmitted() <= mark_ticks`, with mark_ticks captured at line 133 and the rewind an exact-distance StepBackward (back_bars = (Now()-mark_time)/SSR_MSC_PER_MIN, line 144). Nothing in the section bounds the shortfall, so a partial restore - or a restore to the first checkpoint - satisfies both, while the deterministic expectation is that the counter returns to mark_ticks exactly. Confirmed as a weak assertion. Mis-described in magnitude, however, and downgraded from MEDIUM because it is test strength, not engine behaviour / The substance holds but the arithmetic in the evidence is wrong, so the finding is real and mis-described. The bounds at T8:148-155 are TicksEmitted() > 0 and <= mark_ticks, and nothing quantifies the section's own word 'near'; a restore that lands a whole checkpoint interval early passes. But the magnitudes are much smaller than claimed: CSSRReplayClock::Advance scales by speed_x100/100 (SSR_ReplayClock.mqh:94-111), so 900 x Pump(1000) at SSR_SPEED_1 advances 900,000 msc = 15 minutes of replay, and SetTicksPerBar(8) (T8:58) gives roughly 8 synthetic ticks per M1 bar, i.e. mark_ticks on the order of 120 - not 'seven thousand'. Likewise back_bars = (Now()-mark_time)/SSR_MSC_PER_MIN is 5, not 300 (300 pumps = 300,000 msc = 5 minutes). The gap is still real because the default checkpoint interval is 5 minutes of replay time (SSR_SnapshotStore.mqh:33, SSR_CHECKPOINT_INTERVAL_MSC = 5*60*1000): a restore to the 10-minute checkpoint instead of the 15-minute one yields ~80 ticks against mark_ticks ~120 and passes both bounds, because StepBackward's JumpForward re-seeds the intervening bars in bulk rather than as ticks (SSR_ReplayController.mqh:1369-1392, 1452-1458) / Bounds verified, and a tight bound is computable from source, which is what makes this a real gap rather than an unavoidable looseness. SetTicksPerBar(8) and 1x speed give one bar per Pump(1000), so mark_ticks is on the order of 7200 after 900 pumps; SSR_CHECKPOINT_INTERVAL_MSC is 5 minutes and SSR_CHECKPOINT_RING is 64, so 320 bars of coverage means the 300-bar rewind reaches the checkpoint path (not the SeekTo fallback) and StepBackward restores a checkpoint within 5 bars - about 40 ticks - of mark_time. The assertion instead admits [1, mark_ticks], so a restore to the oldest surviving checkpoint, or to a single tick, passes. Severity downgraded from MEDIUM to LOW: the finder concedes the failure the section exists for (a plain seek zeroing the cursor) IS caught by the > 0 bound, the two companion assertions at T8:156-158 independently prove no data survives past the rewind point and the stream stayed ordered, and this is test strength only - no product state is wrong and nothing here touches money or future data

#### `tests-a-13` — T8.5's clamp and no-op assertions read only a return value, and one of them is a hardcoded true

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T8_Navigation.mq5:223`** · category: boundary

```mql5
      Check("jumping to now is a no-op", ctrl.JumpTo(ctrl.Now()));

      //--- out of range must be clamped, not refused into an error
      Check("clamped past the end", ctrl.JumpTo(g_end + 999999999));
```

**What goes wrong:** "jumping to now is a no-op" can never fail: `JumpTo` returns a literal `true` on the equal-target path. "clamped past the end" never reads `ctrl.Now()` afterwards, so it cannot tell a correct clamp to `end_msc` from a clamp to the wrong instant - clamping to `start_msc`, or landing one bar short, would pass while silently rewinding the user's whole session. The section is titled "JumpTo picks the right direction" and the out-of-range case is the one place where the landing point is never checked; every other jump in T8.5 does assert `ctrl.Now()`.

**Why:** `CSSRReplayController::JumpTo` (SSR_ReplayController.mqh) clamps with `long target = SSRClampMsc(target_msc, m_timeline.start_msc, m_timeline.end_msc);` and then has `if(target == m_clock.now_msc) return true;` - an unconditional true for the no-op case - plus `if(delta <= 0) return true;` on the backward path. For the over-range case it falls through to `return (JumpForward(target) >= 0);`. Adding `CheckEq("landed on the end", g_end, ctrl.Now())` (or the bar-open form used at T8:220) would close both.

**Verifiers:** Traced CSSRReplayController::JumpTo (SSR_ReplayController.mqh:1484-1520). With the clock configured (Wire/Load succeeded earlier in T8.5), JumpTo(ctrl.Now()) clamps to the same value, fails `target < m_clock.now_msc`, hits `if(target == m_clock.now_msc) return true;` at line 1517 and returns an unconditional true - so T8:223 is a tautology for any loaded controller. For T8:226, JumpTo(g_end + 999999999) clamps to m_timeline.end_msc and returns (JumpForward(target) >= 0); the section's only follow-up is Status() != SSR_STATE_ERROR (line 227), never ctrl.Now(), so a clamp to the wrong instant - or a short landing - is invisible, unlike every other jump in the section which does assert the landing point (lines 214, 218-220). Confirmed as described; severity IMPROVEMENT since this is missing test coverage, with no evidence the clamp itself is wrong / Both halves check out against CSSRReplayController::JumpTo (SSR_ReplayController.mqh:1484-1512). With a configured clock, JumpTo(ctrl.Now()) clamps to now (SSRClampMsc against a range that contains it) and hits `if(target == m_clock.now_msc) return true;` - an unconditional true, so T8:223 cannot fail for any state the section can reach (the backward path has the same `if(delta <= 0) return true;`). For the over-range case, JumpTo(g_end + 999999999) clamps to end_msc and returns JumpForward(target) >= 0; T8:226-228 then asserts only the bool and Status() != SSR_STATE_ERROR, never ctrl.Now(), so a clamp to the wrong instant - start_msc, which would take the StepBackward branch and also return true - would pass. Every other jump in T8.5 does assert the landing point (T8:216-220), which makes the omission the section's one hole / Both halves verified against CSSRReplayController::JumpTo (SSR_ReplayController.mqh:1484-1519). With the clock configured and now_msc inside [start_msc, end_msc], JumpTo(ctrl.Now()) clamps to now, skips the backward branch and hits 'if(target == m_clock.now_msc) return true;' - an unconditional true, so T8:223 cannot fail (the backward branch likewise returns a literal true when delta <= 0). T8:226 reads only the boolean from the over-range call and never re-reads ctrl.Now(), so a clamp to the wrong endpoint would also pass: clamping to start_msc would take the backward branch and StepBackward returns true. Every other jump in T8.5 does assert the landing point (lines 215, 219, 220-221), which makes the omission visible. Severity LOW is right: the clamp in the code is correct (SSRClampMsc against timeline start/end), so this is missing coverage rather than a live defect; no money arithmetic, no future-data exposure, and the neighbouring check at line 227 still proves the state is not ERROR. Fix is one CheckEq on ctrl.Now() after line 226

#### `tests-b-11` — T9.1's rounding assertions are one-sided and pass when LotForRisk refuses the trade outright

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T9_Trading.mq5:79`** · category: r-multiple

```mql5
      double lot = r.LotForRisk(10000.0, 1.0, 1000.0, 993.0);   // 14.28 lots
      Check("rounded down to the step", lot <= 14.28 + 1e-9,
            StringFormat("%.4f", lot));
      Check("and never above the asked risk",
            r.RiskOf(lot, 7.0) <= 100.0 + 1e-6,
            StringFormat("%.4f", r.RiskOf(lot, 7.0)));
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: T9.1's rounding pair (SSR_T9_Trading.mq5:79-83) asserts only upper bounds, so it proves "never risks more than asked" but not "rounds down to the step": a regression that floors 14.2857 to 14.00 or 10.00 (i.e. under-sizes) would pass. A blanket 0.0 refusal would still be caught by the CheckNear at line 68. Adding an exact CheckNear(14.28, lot, 0.001) closes it; the unguarded direction is conservative, so nothing in the risk guarantee is weakened.

**Why:** Both predicates are upper bounds satisfied by every value in [0, 14.28]. LotForRisk returns 0.0 on four separate refusal paths (SSR_RiskEngine.mqh: dist<=0, balance/risk<=0, per_lot<=0, lot<m_vol_min) and RoundToStep floors, so the true answer here is 14.28 - a figure the test writes in a comment but never asserts. The earlier CheckNear("straightforward size", 10.0, ...) catches a total failure of the function, but not a step- or direction-specific one, which is what this pair is named for.

**Verifiers:** Traced LotForRisk(10000,1,1000,993) in SSR_RiskEngine.mqh:94-131: dist=7, money=100, per_lot=RiskOf(1,7)=7 (tick_size 1.0, tick_value 1.0), lot=RoundToStep(14.2857)=MathFloor(14.2857/0.01+1e-9)*0.01=14.28. Both assertions at SSR_T9_Trading.mq5:79-83 are upper bounds only: lot<=14.28+1e-9 and RiskOf(lot,7)<=100+1e-6. Every value in [0,14.28] satisfies both, including the four refusal returns of 0.0 (lines 100,105,112,123) and any coarser floor (14.00, 10.00). No lower bound and no exact-equality check exists anywhere in T9.1 for this input; the sibling CheckNear at line 68 uses a different input (dist=10, exact 10.0 lots) and so cannot catch a step- or direction-specific regression on the non-exact division. Test-coverage weakness, not a product defect / Arithmetic and predicates check out from source. LotForRisk(10000,1.0,1000,993): dist=7, money=100, per_lot=RiskOf(1,7)=7/1.0*1.0=7, RoundToStep(14.2857)=MathFloor(1428.5714+1e-9)*0.01=14.28 (SSR_RiskEngine.mqh:27-32,94-135). Both assertions at SSR_T9_Trading.mq5:79-83 are upper bounds: `lot <= 14.28+1e-9` and `RiskOf(lot,7) <= 100+1e-6` hold for every value in [0,14.28], including the four 0.0 refusal returns (dist<=0 line 99, balance/risk<=0 line 104, per_lot<=0 line 112, lot<m_vol_min line 120) and any coarser step result. Nothing in the pair distinguishes floor from ceil either: 14.28 is the only value ceil could produce above the bound, but a step change to 1.0 (ceil->15, floor->14) would still pass. The preceding CheckNear(10.0,...) at line 68 pins the divide-and-round pipeline for an exact-step case only, so a direction- or step-specific regression is genuinely unasserted. Test-quality gap, no product defect, hence LOW / Arithmetic checks out: dist=7, money=100, per_lot=RiskOf(1,7)=7, RoundToStep(14.2857)=floor(14.2857/0.01+1e-9)*0.01=14.28 (SSR_RiskEngine.mqh:27-32,110-134). Both predicates at SSR_T9_Trading.mq5:79-83 are upper bounds, satisfied by every value in [0,14.28], so the section named for direction-of-rounding never bounds the result below. But the impact is the reverse of alarming: the unasserted direction is the CONSERVATIVE one (under-sizing risks less than asked), while the safety-critical direction - never above the asked risk - is exactly what the two lines do pin. The finder also overstates reach: a blanket 0.0 regression would be caught one line up by CheckNear("straightforward size", 10.0, ...) at line 68, which uses a different input. What escapes is only a step-granularity or input-specific regression that under-sizes. No user-observable defect, no money corruption; a test-tightening item, not LOW-severity product risk

#### `tests-b-6` — Every Risk().Configure in T9 is discarded by Load; T9's sections silently run on the unknown-symbol fallback specs

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T9_Trading.mq5:112`** · category: input-validation

```mql5
      acct.SetBalance(10000.0);
      acct.Risk().Configure(1.0, 1.0, 0.01, 100.0, 0.01, digits);
      ...
      Check("loaded", ctrl.Load("TEST", start, endt), ctrl.LastErrorText());
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: Accurate as to the mechanism, but the impact is test hygiene only: the six discarded Configure calls are dead code in T9 because the fallback path yields numerically identical tick_size/tick_value, and no T9 assertion after T9.1 reads the risk model. MEDIUM overstates it; this is a LOW latent-trap/dead-setup finding.

**Why:** CSSRReplayController::Load broadcasts OnSessionStart to every observer at SSR_ReplayController.mqh:835-837, and CSSRTradingEngine::OnSessionStart ends with `m_risk.ConfigureFromSymbol(symbol);` (SSR_TradingEngine.mqh:582) - a wholesale overwrite of the six fields Configure() set. ConfigureFromSymbol reads SymbolInfo* and substitutes defaults for every zero (SSR_RiskEngine.mqh:41-57). T9 makes this call before Load in all six of its wired sections (lines 112, 180, 229, 268, 331, 404). T10's Begin() documents the correct order and does the opposite: "set AFTER OnSessionStart, which reconfigures risk from the symbol" (SSR_T10_Statistics.mq5:65-67).

**Verifiers:** Verified the whole chain. CSSRReplayController::Load broadcasts OnSessionStart to every observer (SSR_ReplayController.mqh:834-836), and CSSRTradingEngine::OnSessionStart ends with m_risk.ConfigureFromSymbol(symbol) (SSR_TradingEngine.mqh:582), which unconditionally rewrites all six fields Configure() set (SSR_RiskEngine.mqh:41-57). T9 calls acct.Risk().Configure(...) BEFORE ctrl.Load("TEST",...) in all six wired sections (112, 180, 229, 268, 331, 404), so every one of those Configure calls is discarded. For the non-existent symbol "TEST" the fallbacks yield digits=0, point=MathPow(10,0)=1.0, tick_size=point=1.0, tick_value=1.0, vol 0.01/100/0.01 - and RiskOf/LotForRisk depend only on tick_size and tick_value, so the arithmetic is identical to the intended config and nothing fails today. T9.1, the only section whose assertions actually exercise the risk engine, uses a local CSSRRiskEngine and never calls Load, so it is unaffected / The ordering claim is provable. CSSRReplayController::Load broadcasts OnSessionStart at SSR_ReplayController.mqh:835-837, and CSSRTradingEngine::OnSessionStart ends with m_risk.ConfigureFromSymbol(symbol) at SSR_TradingEngine.mqh:582, wholesale overwriting all six fields Configure() set (SSR_RiskEngine.mqh:41-57 vs 59-70). All six T9 sections call Risk().Configure(...) BEFORE ctrl.Load (lines 112/180/229/268/331/404), and every one asserts Load succeeded (or ignores the return), so the broadcast always runs. T10 does the opposite and says why (SSR_T10_Statistics.mq5:65-67). Severity lowered from MEDIUM: I verified the fallback is numerically identical for everything T9 measures. For an unresolvable symbol every SymbolInfo* call returns 0, so ConfigureFromSymbol yields point=MathPow(10,0)=1.0, tick_size=point=1.0, tick_value=1.0, vol_min=0.01, vol_max=100.0, vol_step=0.01 - the same tick_size/tick_value pair Configure installed, and RiskOf/LotForRisk read only those. digits differs (0 vs 2) but no T9 assertion reads it. So no assertion is masked and no product path is affected; this is dead configuration in a test / Mechanism verified end to end: Load broadcasts OnSessionStart at SSR_ReplayController.mqh:835-837, and CSSRTradingEngine::OnSessionStart ends with m_risk.ConfigureFromSymbol(symbol) (SSR_TradingEngine.mqh:582), wholesale overwriting the six fields Configure() set - and T9 calls Configure BEFORE Load at lines 112,180,229,268,331,404. T10 documents the opposite order in its own comment (SSR_T10_Statistics.mq5:65-67), which corroborates the intent. Impact, however, is nil today and provably so: for an unresolvable "TEST", ConfigureFromSymbol's fallbacks (SSR_RiskEngine.mqh:51-56) give tick_value 1.0, tick_size 1.0, vol_min 0.01, vol_max 100, step 0.01 - identical to Configure's values for everything the maths touches. The only differing fields, m_digits and m_point, are never read by RiskOf/LotForRisk/MoneyOf (grep: they appear only in the two configure methods and the constructor). Nothing the product ships behaves differently; the defect is that six test sections are silently measuring the fallback path, so any future risk/R assertion would be meaningless. MEDIUM overstates a developer-facing fidelity gap with no reachable user effect

#### `tests-b-7` — T9.7's checkpoint-path claim is not asserted; a silent fall back to the plain seek would keep the section green

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T9_Trading.mq5:368`** · category: session-resume

```mql5
      //--- and specifically through the CHECKPOINT path, which is the
      //--- one a real rewind takes once checkpoints exist. ... the snapshot
      //--- path did not, so it gets its own assertion.
      Check("checkpoints were in play", c5.Snapshots().Count() > 0,
            c5.SnapshotText());
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: Correct; the missing assertion is Snapshots().Restores() > 0 (or an increase across the rewind). Severity is test-coverage LOW rather than MEDIUM - no product behaviour is wrong.

**Why:** Count() reports how many checkpoints the ring holds, not that one was restored. StepBackward chooses between two implementations at SSR_ReplayController.mqh:1450-1471: `if(m_snaps.NearestAtOrBefore(target, cp)) { RestoreSnapshot(cp); m_snaps.NoteRestore(); ... } else { ... SeekTo(target) }`. The distinguishing observable is public and unused by the test: CSSRSnapshotStore::Restores() (SSR_SnapshotStore.mqh:90), incremented only on the snapshot branch (NoteRestore, line 162). The path is probably taken today - the first checkpoint is due immediately because m_last_msc starts INVALID (lines 93-98) and the interval is 5 replay minutes (line 33) - which is precisely why the omission is invisible.

**Verifiers:** Traced StepBackward (SSR_ReplayController.mqh:1432-1481): the snapshot branch calls RestoreSnapshot(cp) then m_snaps.NoteRestore(); the else branch logs a warning and calls SeekTo(target). Both branches call PublishRewind(actual) (RestoreSnapshot:1644, SeekTo:1239), and CSSRTradingEngine::OnRewind (SSR_TradingEngine.mqh:634+) is what deletes positions placed after the cut - so T9.7's 'the trade ceased to have existed' and 'open count is back' assertions hold on EITHER path. The only discriminating observable is CSSRSnapshotStore::Restores() (SSR_SnapshotStore.mqh:88, incremented only by NoteRestore at 156), and T9.7 never reads it; Snapshots().Count() (line 84) merely reports filled ring slots, which Checkpoint() fills regardless of any restore. The section's stated purpose (covering the snapshot-restore path specifically) is therefore unasserted / Verified: Count() returns m_count, the number of filled ring slots (SSR_SnapshotStore.mqh:87), which is incremented by Checkpoint() (line 107-108) and says nothing about a restore. StepBackward picks between the snapshot branch and the SeekTo fallback at SSR_ReplayController.mqh:1451-1472, and the only observable that separates them is m_restores, exposed as Restores() (line 90) and bumped solely by NoteRestore() (line 162) on the snapshot branch. I grepped the whole MQL5 tree: Restores() has zero callers, and SnapshotText() (controller:1591) is passed only as the FAIL detail string, which is never printed on a pass. So both of T9.7's rewinds could take the SeekTo fallback with every assertion still green. Severity lowered to LOW: this is a missing assertion in a test, not a defect in shipped behaviour, and the fallback branch is itself correct (it seeks, it just restarts counters) / Verified: StepBackward branches at SSR_ReplayController.mqh:1450-1472 between RestoreSnapshot+NoteRestore and the SeekTo fallback, and CSSRSnapshotStore::Count() (SSR_SnapshotStore.mqh:87) reports ring occupancy only - the branch-distinguishing counter Restores() (line 90, incremented solely in NoteRestore at 162) is public and never touched by T9.7. So the section's stated subject, the snapshot path, is not asserted. Impact is bounded though, which is why MEDIUM is too high: the fallback branch is not a wrong-answer path - it performs a correct SeekTo and the controller itself logs a warning ("no checkpoint before ...; counters will restart", lines 1467-1469), so a silent slide onto it degrades counter continuity rather than corrupting the rewind. The two assertions the phase actually turns on (the trade ceased to exist, open count restored) remain valid on either branch, so no future-data leak or money error can hide here - only loss of coverage over RestoreSnapshot

#### `tests-b-12` — T9.8's "a round trip at a flat price loses money" is satisfied by losing nothing

**`MQL5/Scripts/SSReplay/Tests/SSR_T9_Trading.mq5:433`** · category: commission

```mql5
      Check("a round trip at a flat price loses money",
            a6.Balance() < bal1 + 1e-9,
            DoubleToString(a6.Balance(), 2));
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: The assertion is a non-increase test (true at Balance()==bal1), so the spread and 10-point slippage contributions to the round trip are never bounded below; a favourable-fill regression is caught only if its gain exceeds the 7.0 exit commission that the preceding CheckNear does pin. It is not true that a zero-charging Close() would slip through - that fails the p.commission==14.0 assertion one line above.

**Why:** `a6.Balance() < bal1 + 1e-9` is true when Balance() == bal1. The concrete loss is asserted only for commission, by the preceding CheckNear("commission charged again on exit", 14.0, p.commission, 0.001); the spread and the 10-point slippage contributions to the round trip are never bounded below (an assertion like Balance() <= bal1 - 7.0 - slippage cost would do it).

**Verifiers:** Traced T9.8: bal0 is read before Open (line 417), the entry commission of 7.0 is asserted at 419, bal1 is read after it (428), then Close(t) runs. The predicate at 433-435, a6.Balance() < bal1 + 1e-9, is true when Balance() == bal1, so it is 'balance did not increase', not 'money was lost'. The only lower-bounded cost in the section is the commission (CheckNear 14.0 on p.commission at 431), so the 20-point spread and the 10-point slippage contributions to the round trip are never bounded below anywhere in T9.8. Accurate; test-assertion weakness only / The predicate `a6.Balance() < bal1 + 1e-9` (SSR_T9_Trading.mq5:433-435) is literally satisfied at Balance() == bal1, so it tests non-increase, not loss - that part is exact, and bal1 is indeed read at line 428 after the entry commission was already charged (asserted at line 419). But the finder's stated regression is wrong in context: a Close() that charges nothing would fail the immediately preceding CheckNear("commission charged again on exit", 14.0, p.commission, 0.001) at lines 431-432, so that path is caught. What is genuinely unbounded is the spread/slippage half: with SetSpreadPoints(20) and slippage_points=10 the round trip must cost the spread plus slippage on top of the 7 exit commission, and nothing asserts any lower bound on the balance change, so a favourable-fill regression is only caught if it exceeds the exit commission / The predicate at line 433-434 does admit equality (Balance()==bal1 passes), and bal1 is read after the entry fee, so the line only forbids profit. But the finder's own example regression is refuted by the adjacent assertion: ClosePosition debits the same value it records - m_pos[i].commission += fee and m_balance += realised - fee are the same statement pair (SSR_TradingEngine.mqh:218,222) - so a Close() that charged nothing would break CheckNear("commission charged again on exit", 14.0, p.commission) at line 431. What genuinely escapes is narrower: a regression that zeroes the spread/slippage component of realised (fills both legs at mid) still leaves balance = bal1 - 7 and passes, and the entry check at line 424-426 (open_price >= Ask()) permits zero slippage too. A cost-free round trip is the optimistic-backtest failure mode this phase exists to prevent, so it is worth bounding - but it is a test gap only; the shipped arithmetic is correct as read

#### `spikes-audits-22` — C2's documented manual procedure stops the service 60 seconds before its own run-length gate is satisfied

**`MQL5/Services/SSReplay/Spike/SSR_C2_ServicePersistence.mq5:121`** · category: measurement-validity

```mql5
SSR_Verdict("survived_full_run", uptime_s >= InpRunSec * 0.95,
            IntegerToString(InpRunSec) + "s",
            StringFormat("%.0fs", uptime_s), "");
```

**What goes wrong:** The header's step-by-step procedure ends with 't+300s stop the service' while InpRunSec defaults to 360, and the gate requires uptime >= 342 s. An operator who follows the printed instructions exactly gets IsStopped() at 300 s, an uptime of 300 s, and a FAILED survived_full_run - so the correct execution of the procedure guarantees a failing verdict, and the spike's result cannot be read as evidence either way.

**Why:** Header line 19: 't+300s  stop the service'. Line 28: `input int InpRunSec = 360;`. The loop at line 69 is `while(!IsStopped())` and exits early on operator stop; uptime_s is then computed at line 106 from the real elapsed time. 300 < 360*0.95 = 342, so the verdict fails. The other two verdicts (no_restart, no_long_gaps) are unaffected, which makes the failure look like a partial result rather than an instruction/default mismatch.

**Verifiers:** Arithmetic and control flow both check out. Header line 19 instructs 't+300s stop the service'; InpRunSec defaults to 360 (line 28); the loop is while(!IsStopped()) (line 69) with an internal break only at uptime >= InpRunSec*1000.0 ms (line 97), so an operator stop at 300 s exits via IsStopped(). uptime_s is then computed from real elapsed microseconds (line 106) and the gate at line 121 requires uptime_s >= 360*0.95 = 342, so 300 < 342 FAILs deterministically for anyone following the printed procedure. no_restart and no_long_gaps are computed independently and are unaffected, matching the claim. Downgraded to LOW: this is a header/default mismatch in a spike harness, fixed either by changing the printed t+300s or the InpRunSec default, and it produces a false FAIL rather than a false PASS / Purely arithmetic and provable from the file. Header line 19 instructs 't+300s stop the service'; line 28 is `input int InpRunSec = 360;`; the loop at line 69 is while(!IsStopped()) and breaks early when the operator stops it, after which uptime_s is computed from the real elapsed microsecond count at line 106; the gate at line 121 is uptime_s >= InpRunSec * 0.95 = 342. So an operator who follows the printed procedure exactly gets ~300 s and a FAILED survived_full_run, while the other two verdicts pass - which reads as a partial result rather than as an instruction/default mismatch. Severity lowered to LOW: this is a false FAIL in spike code, visible immediately and fixable by one number, not a false PASS that could mislead a design decision / Verified: header line 19 is 't+300s stop the service', line 28 is input int InpRunSec = 360, and line 121 gates survived_full_run on uptime_s >= InpRunSec * 0.95 = 342 s. The loop at 69 is while(!IsStopped()) and exits as soon as the operator stops it, after which uptime_s is computed from real elapsed time at line 106, so following the printed procedure exactly yields ~300 s and a guaranteed FAILED verdict while the other two verdicts pass - which reads as a partial result rather than an instruction/default mismatch. Severity downgraded from MEDIUM to LOW on impact: the failure is self-explaining because SSR_Verdict prints expected '360s' beside actual '300s', so the arithmetic is visible in the result row; an operator who simply lets the service run to its own break at line 97 gets a PASS; and this is a Tier-C measurement spike with no product surface - no money arithmetic, no future-data leak, no weakening of the virtual-trading guarantee. One-line fix either way (InpRunSec = 300, or amend the header to t+360s)

#### `spikes-audits-29` — Audit A9 cannot see a duplicated pointer-returning method, the exact shape DECL_METHOD was fixed for

**`tools/ssr_audit.py:425`** · category: audit-coverage

```mql5
METHOD_DEF = re.compile(r'^\s*(?:virtual\s+|static\s+|const\s+)*'
                        r'[A-Za-z_][\w:]*\s*[\*&]?\s+'
                        r'([A-Za-z_]\w*)\s*\(([^)]*)\)')
```

**What goes wrong** *(as corrected by the verifiers)*: Confirmed for the primary claim. One sub-claim is wrong: A9's class-scope bookkeeping is NOT affected. Scope is tracked purely by per-line brace counting (lines 552-582, depth/cls_depth/entered) and by CLASS_HEAD, never by METHOD_DEF, so a pointer-returning declaration A9 cannot parse does not disturb the depth==1 window or the `seen` map — duplicates of other methods after it are still detected normally. The defect is only the missed shape itself (and the truncated argument key on default arguments containing parentheses).

**Why:** Line 426 is `r'[A-Za-z_][\w:]*\s*[\*&]?\s+'` - a mandatory \s+ after the star. The comment at lines 343-345 records the same bug being fixed in DECL_METHOD: 'the star hugs the NAME in this codebase - CSSRBarProvider *Bars(void) - so whitespace after it must be optional, or every pointer-returning method reads as undeclared. Three false positives said so immediately.' DECL_METHOD (line 346-349) uses `\s*[\*&]?\s*`; METHOD_DEF was not updated. METHOD_DEF's argument group `([^)]*)` additionally stops at the first ')', so a default argument containing parentheses ends the match.

**Verifiers:** Verified by running the regex: METHOD_DEF's mandatory \s+ after the optional [\*&] means '   CSSRBarProvider *Bars(void)' does not match (no backtracking path succeeds — the only whitespace sits before the star, and the name group cannot start on '*'). '   CSSRBarProvider* Bars(void)' does match, but the tree uses the star-hugs-the-name form exclusively: 23 such declarations exist (SSR_IDataSource.mqh:217-219, SSR_Mt5DataSource.mqh:80-81,91,142-144, SSR_MemoryDataSource.mqh:264-266,299, SSR_ReplayController.mqh:617-618,1590, SSR_TradingEngine.mqh:566, SSR_ChartManager.mqh:191, SSR_GroupPort.mqh:66, etc.), and audit_a9 (line 562) is the only consumer, so a second definition of any of them inside one class would be invisible to A9 while MetaEditor reports 'member function already defined'. The ([^)]*) truncation on a default argument containing parentheses is also real (verified: 'bool Load(const int a, const int b=f(1))' keys on the truncated arg string). The contrast with DECL_METHOD (line 348, '\s*[\*&]?\s*') and its comment at 343-345 is exactly as quoted / Verified by executing the regex. METHOD_DEF (ssr_audit.py:425-427) is `[A-Za-z_][\w:]*\s*[\*&]?\s+` — the `\s+` after the optional star is mandatory, so `   CSSRBarProvider *Bars(void)` and `   CSSRBarProvider  *Bars(void)` both return None, while `   CSSRBarProvider* Bars(void)` matches. The codebase uses the star-hugs-name form in 16 places (SSR_TradingEngine.mqh:566, SSR_StrategyHost.mqh:99, SSR_ChartManager.mqh:191, SSR_ReplayController.mqh:617-618,1590, SSR_MasterClock.mqh:89, SSR_IDataSource.mqh:93, SSR_Mt5DataSource.mqh:142-144, SSR_CustomSymbolSink.mqh:128,132, SSR_GroupPort.mqh:66, ...), so A9 cannot see a duplicate of any of them — exactly the 'member function already defined' shape A9 exists for, and exactly the bug the comment at 343-345 records fixing in DECL_METHOD (346-349, which correctly uses `\s*[\*&]?\s*`). The `([^)]*)` argument group also truncates at the first ')', so a default argument containing parens yields a wrong key. Tool-coverage gap only, no shipped defect today, hence LOW / The regex gap is real and I reproduced it: METHOD_DEF's mandatory `\s+` after the optional `[\*&]?` means `CSSRBarProvider *Bars(void)` never matches (tested; `CSSRBarProvider* Bars(void)` does). 16 declarations in 11 files use the star-hugs-name style the tree actually writes, so a duplicate of any of them is invisible to A9 — the exact accident A9 was written for, and with no MT5 compiler in the build environment an audit blind spot is the only line of defence. BUT the second half of the claim is false: I ran A9's loop over a class containing a duplicated pointer method AND a duplicated void method, and it still fired on the void duplicate. Class-scope bookkeeping is brace-counting on every line, independent of METHOD_DEF, so nothing 'is found only by luck'. Impact-wise nothing is user-observable: no shipped behaviour changes, the cost is one MetaEditor round trip if a future patch duplicates a pointer-returning getter. LOW, not higher
